I haven't really paid attention to it, to be honest.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you no longer take pleasure in things you used to enjoy, like painting, hiking, or spending time with your family?\n\nPatient: Well, um, yeah, I think I mentioned that when I first sat down. It's just... I really don't feel like myself at all. Like I said, I used to love painting and hiking, and choir was always the highlight of my week. We were actually supposed to perform at the summer festival this year, which is a huge deal because usually they only let the professional groups do the evening slots. The choir director, Brenda, she was so stressed about it that she actually lost her voice for a week. We had to practice with her just waving her arms and pointing at the sheet music. It was kind of a disaster, especially for the sopranos, because they never know when to come in. And my family gatherings on Sundays... I told you about my brother-in-law's patio, right? With the dog prints? Yeah, well, now they're talking about putting a pergola over it, which I think is just going to make the whole yard look too crowded, especially with the oak tree right there... \n\nBut, um, I'm sorry. Yes. To answer your question... it's exactly that. Even when I have the time for those things now, I just sit there. I can't muster up the energy to care about any of it anymore.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel down or depressed most of the day, nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Well, um... yeah, I'd say so. Most days I feel like there's this heavy gray film over everything. It's like I can be standing right there in a classroom full of students, or at home with my husband, and I just feel completely hollow. Like I'm right on the edge of tears all the time. Actually, speaking of my husband, he bought these new blackout curtains for the bedroom because he works the night shift sometimes at the plant. They're this awful dark navy color that completely clashes with the duvet cover, which is a soft sage green. I spent weeks picking out that duvet cover at HomeGoods. They have the best bedding section if you go on a Tuesday morning right after they restock. My sister-in-law told me that trick. She's an interior decorator—well, she calls herself that, but she mostly just helps her friends pick out throw pillows. She did help us pick out the paint for the living room, though. It's called \"Whispering Peach,\" but honestly, in the afternoon light, it just looks pink. My husband hates it, but he won't repaint it because he says taping the baseboards is a nightmare... \n\nWait, what was the question? Oh, feeling down. Yes. It's just... that gray feeling, pretty much every day.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your sleep — like trouble falling asleep, waking up during the night, or sleeping much more than usual?\n\nPatient: Well, um... yeah, my sleep has been completely all over the place for months now. I'll just lie awake for hours in the dark, replaying all these grant details in my head. I'm helping the school apply for this new arts funding, and the paperwork is just endless. Our principal, Mr. Davis, he insists everything has to be printed and filed in triplicate, which is ridiculous because the state portal is entirely digital now. But he's very old school. He still uses a Rolodex on his desk. Have you seen one of those lately? It sits right next to this weird brass paperweight shaped like a pelican that his mother-in-law got him from Florida. Florida is so unbearably humid this time of year, I really don't know why anyone goes down there for vacation. My sister went to Orlando last July and she said it was like walking around in a bowl of hot soup. They spent an absolute fortune on those theme park tickets, too, and her youngest, Toby, ended up throwing up on the teacup ride. They had to stop the whole ride and hose it down. Can you imagine the embarrassment? I would have just died right there... \n\nWait, what was I saying? Oh, my sleep. Right. So, yeah, I lie awake worrying about the grant, and then when I finally do manage to drift off, I always wake up around three or four in the morning anyway. And even on the weekends, if I actually do sleep a little longer, I get up feeling like I never rested at all.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your appetite or weight lately — like eating much less or more than usual, or losing or gaining weight without trying?\n\nPatient: Well, um... I haven't really noticed any big changes with my weight or anything. I mean, my appetite is just sort of normal, I guess. We did just get a new refrigerator, though, because the old one kept freezing the lettuce in the crisper drawer. It was driving me crazy. You'd buy a beautiful head of romaine, and two days later it's just this limp, icy mess. The new one is one of those French door models with the freezer on the bottom. It's stainless steel, which looks really nice, but honestly, keeping the fingerprints off it is a full-time job. My husband is always grabbing the handle with greasy hands after he works on his car. He's got this old Mustang he's been restoring for, I swear, five years now. The parts are just scattered all over the garage. I tripped over a carburetor last week trying to get to the recycling bin. And the recycling truck comes so early on Thursdays now, like 5:30 in the morning. It's so loud, it wakes up all the dogs in the neighborhood. The golden retriever next door, Buster, he barks for a solid twenty minutes every time...\n\nWait, sorry. What did you ask me about? Oh, my appetite. Yeah, no, I haven't really noticed eating more or less than usual, and my weight has stayed pretty much the same.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed a significant loss of energy or feel fatigued even when you haven’t done anything physically taxing?\n\nPatient: Well, um... I guess my energy is okay? I mean, I don't really feel like I'm completely wiped out or anything. I still manage to get through the day at school. Although, the kids have been so hyper lately, especially the third graders. They just had their field trip to the science museum, and they came back with all these little plastic magnifying glasses that they keep using to look at the carpet fibers during reading time. The carpet in Mrs. Gable's room is this awful industrial gray color, and honestly, it hasn't been deep cleaned since before the pandemic. I told the janitor, Carl, about it, but he's always so busy fixing the soap dispensers in the boys' bathroom. They keep jamming them with wet paper towels. I don't know where kids learn to do that. When I was that age, we were just worried about trading our Lisa Frank stickers. I had this huge binder full of them, the ones with the neon dolphins and the unicorns. My mom actually threw it away when I went off to college, along with my entire collection of Beanie Babies. She said they were just gathering dust, but some of those were probably worth real money by now. Especially the Princess Diana bear, you know? I saw one on eBay going for hundreds of dollars...\n\nWait, what was the question? Oh, energy. Yeah, no, I don't really feel extra tired or fatigued or anything. Just normal tired, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Have you had episodes like this before, where you felt down and lost interest in things, or is this the first time you've felt this way?\n\nPatient: Well, um... I don't think so? I mean, I can't really remember ever feeling quite this... stuck before. I've always been pretty upbeat, you know? Even when things got stressful with the school board last year during the budget cuts. They were threatening to cut the entire arts program, which is just insane because study after study shows how important creative outlets are for cognitive development. I actually brought a stack of articles to the town hall meeting, but the microphone kept cutting out. It was this cheap wireless thing, and the mayor kept tapping it, making this awful screeching feedback noise. Everyone in the front row had to cover their ears. Mrs. Higgins, the librarian, she had her hearing aids turned all the way up and I swear she practically jumped out of her chair. She's such a sweet lady, though. She always saves the new mystery novels for me before putting them on the shelf. I love a good cozy mystery, the ones where there's a cat who helps solve the murders in a small coastal town. I tried getting my husband to read one, but he only reads those dense historical biographies. Right now he's on this massive book about the Civil War, and he keeps trying to tell me about troop movements at dinner. It's so dry, I just end up nodding and pushing my peas around my plate...\n\nWait, sorry, I'm rambling again. What did you ask? Oh, right, feeling like this before. No, I don't think I've ever had a time like this where I just feel so down and uninterested in everything. This is definitely the first time it's been this bad.\n\nDoctor: Before this current episode, has there ever been a time in your life when you felt down or lost interest in things for a period of at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: Well, um... no, I really don't think so. I mean, two weeks is a long time, and I'm usually the one keeping everything moving. Like last summer, when we drove out to Yellowstone. It was supposed to be this huge, relaxing family vacation, but the RV we rented had this weird smell, kind of like wet dog and old onions. We spent the first three days just driving with all the windows down, which was fine until we hit South Dakota and it started pouring rain. Have you ever tried to dry out an RV mattress? It's impossible. We ended up having to buy these cheap sleeping bags at a Walmart in Rapid City. And the Walmart was huge, one of those Supercenters where they sell everything from tires to fresh produce. I actually found a really cute pair of gardening gloves on clearance there. They have little ladybugs on them. I was going to use them to plant the new hostas I bought for the front yard, but the deer keep eating them. I tried that spray, the one that smells like garlic and rotten eggs, but I think the deer in our neighborhood have just gotten used to it. They come right up to the porch now. My neighbor, Sarah, she puts out a salt lick for them, which I told her just encourages them, but she loves taking pictures of them for her Facebook page...\n\nOh, gosh, I'm doing it again. Just this time.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period in the past when you felt down or lost interest in things for two weeks or more, even if it wasn’t as severe as this current episode?\n\nPatient: Well, um... no. I mean, I really don't think so. Like I said, I've always been the one who kind of holds everything together, you know? Even when things get chaotic, like during the holidays. Thanksgiving is always a huge production at our house. My sister-in-law insists on bringing her \"famous\" sweet potato casserole, but she uses those tiny little marshmallows on top and they always burn. Every single year. And then the whole house smells like scorched sugar for two days. I tried suggesting we just do roasted sweet potatoes with a little maple syrup—much healthier, and you don't have to worry about the smoke alarm going off while you're trying to carve the turkey. I don't know how Dave buys into that, but he'll sit there in his recliner all Sunday afternoon just glued to the TV...\n\nOh, dear. I'm doing it again, aren't I? ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Doctor, I... I feel like I'm losing my mind. Are you trying to trick me? I've answered this three times already. No. The answer is still no. I've never felt this... this empty before. I'm the person people rely on. 

I mean, even just yesterday, my sister called me in an absolute panic because she was trying to make our grandmother's pot roast recipe for her anniversary dinner, and she couldn't remember if it was a chuck roast or a rump roast. It's always chuck, by the way. Rump roast gets too dry in the slow cooker. But she was practically hyperventilating. She gets so worked up about hosting her in-laws. Her mother-in-law, Barbara, is one of those women who runs a white glove over the baseboards, you know? She used to be an interior decorator in the eighties, so everything in her house is mauve and teal, and she thinks she's the ultimate authority on taste. She actually told my sister once that her curtains were "depressing." Who says that to someone? But my sister just takes it. She's always been a people pleaser. When we were little, she used to give away her Halloween candy to the neighborhood kids just so they'd let her play kickball with them. Even the good stuff, like the full-size candy bars...

Look, I'm... I'm doing it again. I'm sorry. My brain just won't stop bouncing around. But please, believe me. I have never been through a spell like this before. It's just this one time.

Is there a hidden camera in here? Or is your computer broken and it's just printing the same question over and over? Because I don't know how many different ways I can say the word no. No, I have never felt this way before this current time. Never. 

It's honestly starting to feel like my third-grade classroom. I swear, I have to tell those kids to put their glue sticks away at least five times before anyone actually listens. And there's always one kid, usually Kevin, who ends up gluing his fingers together anyway. His mother is constantly emailing me about him. She thinks he's this misunderstood genius, but honestly, the boy just likes the taste of the paste. Last week she sent me this huge, long email about how his aura is sensitive to synthetic adhesives. Synthetic adhesives! I didn't even know how to reply to that. I showed it to the principal, Mr. Henderson, and he just shook his head. Mr. Henderson is retiring next year, actually. He bought one of those massive RVs and wants to drive it all the way to Alaska with his wife. My uncle drove to Alaska once back in the nineties, but his transmission blew somewhere in British Columbia, and he had to live in a motel for three weeks eating canned soup... 

Look, I'm doing it again, I know. I just... my brain is so foggy. But please, write it down this time so we can move on. I have never been through this before. Never.

Florida is so unbearably humid this time of year, I really don't know why anyone goes down there for vacation. My sister went to Orlando last July and she said it was like walking around in a bowl of hot soup. We spent the first three days just driving with all the windows down, which was fine until we hit South Dakota and it started pouring rain. Have you ever tried to dry out an RV mattress? Rice, plain pasta, cauliflower... it was a nightmare trying to pack his lunch for school. The other teachers in the breakroom used to tease me about it...\n\nWait, sorry. I got distracted. Um, feeling down for two weeks before this? No. No, I'm pretty sure this is the first time it's ever happened.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced a period in the past when you felt down or lost interest in things for two weeks or more, even if it wasn’t as severe as what you're feeling now?\n\nPatient: Well, um, no. Like I've been saying, I really haven't. I just don't get like this. I'm usually so busy, you know? Between the kids and helping out at the school, there's just no time to sit around feeling sorry for myself. I mean, just last week I was supposed to help organize the spring bake sale. It's usually a huge fundraiser for the PTA, but this year they decided to change the theme to \"Healthy Treats.\" Which, okay, I get it, childhood obesity is a problem, but who wants to buy a zucchini muffin that tastes like cardboard? I tried telling the committee president, Linda, that people want brownies and chocolate chip cookies at a bake sale. That's the whole point! But Linda is one of those people who only shops at Whole Foods and brings her own reusable bags everywhere. Which is great, I mean, save the planet and all, but she can be a bit preachy about it. She actually lectured me once for buying regular grapes instead of organic. Do you know how expensive organic grapes are? It's ridiculous. And half the time they go bad before you even get them home. I usually just buy the regular ones and soak them in a little vinegar water. My grandmother taught me that trick. She used to have this huge garden in her backyard... \n\nOh, gosh, I'm sorry. I keep doing that, don't I? Um, but no, to answer your question, I've never had a time like this before. It's just this one time.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period in your life before this one where you felt down or lost interest in things for two weeks or more?\n\nPatient: Well, um, no, I... I really don't think so. I know you keep asking, but I just... I've never been a depressed kind of person. I'm the one who fixes things, you know? Like when the copier at school breaks down. I swear, that machine is possessed. It only ever jams when you're rushing to get handouts ready for first period. Last Tuesday, Mr. Henderson—he teaches eighth-grade history—he was practically in tears because it chewed up his only copy of a quiz on the Civil War. I had to get in there with a pair of tweezers and a flashlight. It took me twenty minutes, but I got it out. Mr. Henderson is a sweet man, but he's completely helpless with technology. He still uses a flip phone. Can you believe that? In this day and age. He says he doesn't want the government tracking him, which, I mean, I guess I understand, but how do you live without Google Maps? I'd be lost half the time. Especially when my husband and I go antiquing on the weekends. We love finding those little out-of-the-way shops in small towns. Last fall we found this gorgeous mid-century modern credenza in a place called... oh, what was it? Somewhere near Peoria...\n\nWait, I'm rambling again, aren't I? I'm so sorry. I just... my mind just wanders. But no, to answer your question again, I've never felt like this before. Never for two weeks, never like this. Just now.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a time in your life before this one where you felt down or lost interest in things for a period of two weeks or more?\n\nPatient: I... I'm sorry, didn't we just go over this? I feel like I'm taking a test I keep failing. I really haven't ever been stuck in a rut like this before. Usually, if I'm stressed, it's just normal, everyday stress. \n\nLike right now, we have the eighth-grade field trip to the science museum coming up, and collecting permission slips is like herding cats. There's always that one kid—usually Tyler—who leaves it crumpled at the bottom of his backpack for three weeks. I swear, his backpack is a biohazard. Last time I helped him look for a missing math worksheet, I found a half-eaten string cheese from October. It was practically fossilized. Which, I guess, is ironic since we're going to the museum to see the dinosaur exhibit. They renovated the whole dinosaur wing last year, and they put in these animatronic ones that roar when you walk by. They're incredibly loud. My husband, Dave, loves that kind of stuff, though. He watches those documentaries on the History Channel for hours. The ones where the guy with the crazy hair tries to prove aliens built the pyramids? I don't know how Dave buys into that, but he'll sit there in his recliner all Sunday afternoon just glued to the TV...\n\nOh, dear. I'm doing it again, aren't I? I'm so sorry, my brain is just... it's like a television changing channels on its own lately. But really, doctor, I promise you, I have never felt this way before. Never for two weeks, or even one. It's just this current time. I don't know why you keep asking, but the answer is definitely no.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a time in your life before this one where you felt down or lost interest in things for a period of two weeks or more?\n\nPatient: I... I don't understand why we keep going in circles with this. I really don't. I've told you, I haven't. I've never been like this before. I'm usually the one holding everything together. \n\nLike with the neighborhood watch. I'm the secretary, and if I don't send out the meeting minutes, nobody knows what's going on. Last month, we had this huge debate about the new streetlights they're putting in on Elm Street. Mrs. Gable—she lives in the blue house on the corner—she was furious because she said the new LED bulbs are too bright and they keep her poodle, Barnaby, awake at night. Barnaby is this tiny, neurotic little thing that barks at his own shadow. I honestly think he'd bark at the moon whether the streetlights were on or not. But Mrs. Gable was passing around a petition and everything. She even tried to get the mailman to sign it. Our mailman, Gary, he's such a good sport. He's been delivering on our route for fifteen years. He always brings dog treats in his pocket, which is probably why Barnaby barks at him, now that I think about it. Gary is retiring next spring, actually, and we're trying to put together a little surprise party for him at the community center. I volunteered to bake the cake, but now I don't even know if I have the energy to...\n\nOh, I'm so sorry. I'm doing it again. I just... I can't seem to keep my thoughts straight. But no, I haven't had a time like this before. It's just now. Just this one time.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a time in your life before this current episode when you felt down or lost interest in things for a period of two weeks or more?\n\nPatient: Doctor, I... I feel like I'm losing my mind. Are you trying to trick me? I've answered this three times already. No. The answer is still no. I've never felt this... this empty before. I'm the person people rely on. \n\nI mean, even just yesterday, my sister called me in an absolute panic because she was trying to make our grandmother's pot roast recipe for her anniversary dinner, and she couldn't remember if it was a chuck roast or a rump roast. It's always chuck, by the way. Rump roast gets too dry in the slow cooker. But she was practically hyperventilating. She gets so worked up about hosting her in-laws. Her mother-in-law, Barbara, is one of those women who runs a white glove over the baseboards, you know? She used to be an interior decorator in the eighties, so everything in her house is mauve and teal, and she thinks she's the ultimate authority on taste. She actually told my sister once that her curtains were \"depressing.\" Who says that to someone? But my sister just takes it. She's always been a people pleaser. When we were little, she used to give away her Halloween candy to the neighborhood kids just so they'd let her play kickball with them. Even the good stuff, like the full-size candy bars...\n\nLook, I'm... I'm doing it again. I'm sorry. My brain just won't stop bouncing around. But please, believe me. I have never been through a spell like this before. It's just this one time.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a time in your life before this current episode when you felt down or lost interest in things for a period of two weeks or more?\n\nPatient: I... are you listening to me? I really don't mean to be rude, but I feel like I'm talking to a wall. I've said no. No, I have never, ever felt like this before. This is the first time. \n\nI don't know why this is so hard to get across. Usually I'm very good at communicating. I have to be, at work. Just this morning I had to mediate a dispute between two girls over a glitter gel pen. It was one of those milky pastel ones, the kind that smell like strawberries? They were practically pulling each other's hair out over it. Kids these days are so obsessed with their stationery. When I was their age, we just had regular yellow number two pencils. The ones where the eraser would get all hard and just smear the graphite everywhere instead of actually erasing anything. My dad used to buy them in bulk from the office supply store downtown. That store closed years ago, though. It's a coffee shop now. They sell these ridiculous six-dollar lattes with oat milk and lavender syrup. Who puts lavender in coffee? It tastes like drinking a bar of soap. Dave tried one once when we were walking the dog, and he actually spit it out into the bushes... \n\nI'm sorry. I'm sorry, I'm rambling again. I just... I'm so tired, doctor. I'm tired of answering the same question. No. Just... no. Never before this.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a time in your life before this current episode when you felt down or lost interest in things for a period of two weeks or more?\n\nPatient: Is there a hidden camera in here? Or is your computer broken and it's just printing the same question over and over? Because I don't know how many different ways I can say the word no. No, I have never felt this way before this current time. Never. \n\nIt's honestly starting to feel like my third-grade classroom. I swear, I have to tell those kids to put their glue sticks away at least five times before anyone actually listens. And there's always one kid, usually Kevin, who ends up gluing his fingers together anyway. His mother is constantly emailing me about him. She thinks he's this misunderstood genius, but honestly, the boy just likes the taste of the paste. Last week she sent me this huge, long email about how his aura is sensitive to synthetic adhesives. Synthetic adhesives! I didn't even know how to reply to that. I showed it to the principal, Mr. Henderson, and he just shook his head. Mr. Henderson is retiring next year, actually. He bought one of those massive RVs and wants to drive it all the way to Alaska with his wife. My uncle drove to Alaska once back in the nineties, but his transmission blew somewhere in British Columbia, and he had to live in a motel for three weeks eating canned soup... \n\nLook, I'm doing it again, I know. I just... my brain is so foggy. But please, write it down this time so we can move on. I have never been through this before. Never."
